The Japanese Ambassador presents his compliments
894.3311
to the Honorable the Seeretary of State and has the honor
to inform him that the Japanese transports "HAYATOMO",
"ONDO", and "IRoO" wish to visit Los Angeles for the purpose
of taking on crude petroleum, and Honolulu in order to re-
plenish their supply of water and food, according to the
3
following soheduley
A
"HAYATOMO"
Los Angeles Arrive April 18 Leave April 22
Honolulu
Arrive May 2
Leave May 5
"ONDO"
Los Angeles Arrive May 6
Leave May 10
Honolulu
Arrive May 21
Leave May 24
"IROO"
MAR 30 193
Los Angeles Arrive July 2
Leave July 6
PTT
Honolulu
Arrive July 17
Leave July 20
In placing the above information at the Secretary
disposal, the Ambassador begs to be informed whether the
proposed visits and the landing of the orew would be agree-
able to the United States Government, and whether the vessel
would be given by the United States Government the courtesies
and facilities usual in such cases.
F/FG
C. C.
March 26, 1938.

Tokyo Higher Meroantile Marine Sohool (Tokyo Koto Shosen

894.3311/467

ruthless bombing of defenseless Chinese cities by the

officials for arranging an official welcome for the
Japanese training ship at a time when the American
people are taking a firm stand in defense of peace and
democracy arainst fascist agcrcssors.

TAH LASI: DU ALLAIND
The Secretary of State.
Dear Lir. Secretary:
Reference is made to your letter of July 5, 1938
(PR), and my reply of July 9, 1938, concerning the visit of
the Japanese training ship TAISEI rARU of the Tokyo Higher
894.3311/481
Mercantile Marine School to Portland, Oregon, fron August 5
to August 13, 1938.
Information received in the Department from Fort
Stevens, Oregon, indicates that the Japanese ship TAISEI MiRU
passed Fort Stevens at 11: 55 A.Li., August 4, 1938. The ship
did not salute so no salute was fired from that station. The
usual boarding visit was made by an officer of the station,
but the visit was not returned, the captain stating that he
was not coming ashore but that he might upon his return to
Astoria from Fortland.
Local authorities have been reliably informed that
the TAISEI LuRU is not a Japenese government vessel but is
privately owned and commanded by a merchant marine captain,
which probably accounts for the fact that the usual courtesies
were not paid. It was noted that the American flag was not
displayed at the fore peak as is generally customary while
foreign ships are in merican harbors.
Sincerely yours,
14
Secretary of War.

NoWa
Sir:
Acknowledgment is made of State Department note
of August 26, 1938, file PR 894.3311/481, referring to a com-
munication from the Acting Secretary of War :hich stated that
the TAISEI MARU of the Tokyo Higher Mercantile Marine School
894.33111/48
passed Fort Stevens without tendering a salute; that the boarding
visit made by an officer of the United States Army Station was
not returned; and, that the American flag was. not displayed at
the fore peak while the ship was in the harbor.
I am pleased to advise you that, according to information
available to this Department, the TAISEI MARU is not a Japanese
Government vessel, and is not a saluting ship; that "boarding"
visits, as such, are not usually returned by merchant vessels; and,
that while such vessels usually display the flag of the country
being visited at the fore there is no obligation in this respect.
2
It is the view of the Navy Department that it may be asgumed that
no discourtesy or omission was intended by the captain of'the TAISEI
MARU.
Respectfuliy,
MOK. turlong
The Honorable,
Acting ocretary the Navy.
The Secretary of State.

Mr. woodward:
IN has no suggestion in regard to PR's draft in
894.3311
except that PR may want to take into account tre follow-
ing factors.
894.3311/
According to a voluntary reoort on the subject of
Training Personnel for the Japanese Merchant Marine by
American Vice Consul Beppo R. Johansen at Yokohama,
August 11, 1938, Japanese training ships may carry cargo
"when necessary for the training work", hence the carrying
507
of cargo by the ship is adecuately covered by the educa-
tional system for merchant officers and seamen and might
become the basis for a reply by the Jananese authorities
if objection is entered to a training vessel taking on or
carrying cargo. On the other hand, no vessel may load
cargo for a foreign vort without delivering to the Collector
of Customs a manifest of all cargo on board which must be
done before clearance can be ranted. If the vessel failed
F
A
to obtain clearance, the Master is l1able to a fine of 500
to

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to $1oo0. The Commerce Department is asking for a report
on this case from the Collector of Customs at Honolulu.
To this extent tre vessel did not comply with the United
States Navigation Laws unless it is considered that such
laws were suspended by the courtesy arrangements made.
Finally, the Maritime Commission is submitting a
report to Congress on January 3, 1939 on the subject of
training of seamen and officers for merchant ships. This
report will use the Japanese training system as the most
complete example of training for sea-going careers and will
include, with certain deletions, the entire American
consular report referred to.
At the present moment the Maritime Commission 1s in-
vestigating great practices of two Japanese lines operating
to and from American ports.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Department of State
894.3311
I have the honor to refer to your letter or the
29th ultimo, file PR, wherein you advise of the receipt
of a note dated April 27th from the Japanese Ambassador
to the effect that the Japanese Training Squadron. con-
sisting of the coast defense ships Yakumo and Iwate,
propose to visit Honolulu, Hawaii, from October 18th
to October 23rd; San Francisco, California, from
November lst to November 7th; San Pedro, California,
from November 9th to November 13th and Hilo, Hawaii,
894.3311/525
from November 22nd to November 26th, 1939.
You are informed that our appropriate field officers
today are being advised of the above, and are being re-
quested to extend the personnel of the vessels every
courtesy consistent with the enforcement of the immigra-
tion laws and regulations.
Respectfully.
EDW. J. SHAUGHNESSY,
Deputy Commisstoner.
ADVISER
MR. HORNBECK

My dear Mr. Secretary:
I have the honor to acknowledge receipt of your
letter of the 17th instant, file PR 894.3311/528
wherein you advise of the receipt of a note dated
May 12th from the Japanese Ambassador, stating that
the Japanese training ships, Nihon Maru and the Kaio
Maru. of the Training School for Navigation of the
894.3311/530
Department of Education of the Japanese Government,
would leave Tokyo on May 17th, on a cruise for train-
ing in ocean navigation, and propose to visit San
Francisco, California, from July 3 to July 1lth, and
Hild, Hawai1, from August 2 to August 9, 1939. It
is also stated that the ships are sailing boats equipped
with auxiliary engines but will for the most part
navigate under sail; consequently, their schedule might
be subject to some slight changes according to weather
conditions; that the Nihon Maru will be @ommanded by
Captain Teitoku Nishizama and the Kaio Maru by Captain
Jiro Hadano, each ship having on board a crew of 45,
13 teachers and 50 students.
Our San Francisco and Honolulu offices are today
being advised of the above, and are being asked to
LHO
extend the usual courtesies on such occasions to the
personnel involved.
Respectfully,
SHAUGHNDSST.

August 29, 1910.
Sir:
I have the honor to quote the following from reports re-
ceived by the Navy Department regarding visits of Japanese vessels
to the Philippine Islands:-
"Report that a Japanese squadron consiting of the
8
IN
first olass cruiser "AZUMA? and the armored oruiser "TOKIw▲"
6
under command of Vioe Admiral Nakana, visited Cobu June 17-
h
21, for the purpose of taking on fresh water and provisions.
Our agents at Cebu report that two launches from the ω
war ships took soundings of the North Channel two nights in
3
succession, guided by the searchlight of the "AZUMA", and that
their operations lacted until eleven o'clook on the first night
and one o'clock in the morning the second.
8
Major R.S. Bowman, Philippine Scouts, now on reoruit
ing duty at Cebu in reply to a letter from this office reports
on this visit as follows:-
'Reference your letter of June 14, 1919, report
that the squadron mentioned arrived here about 1:O0 p.m., Juno
17th. Were sighted at about l0:00 o'clock but they went around
Mactan and came in through the south ohannel, one of them tying
up at the dock near the Custom House. It was reported to me
that they did soundings in the north channel during the first
night here. I saw one boat returning from the direction of
Maotan, straight east of the custom house, on the afternoon of
the 17th. They visited the Visayan Refining Plant at Opon, went
over the Toledo Road, visited Carcar by train, and both officers
and men covered every street in Cebu, the men apparently under
orders. They gave a tea yesterday afternoon and had a reception
last night at the Cebu Hotel. Many of the cadets carried cameras,
but I did not see or hear of any being used. I heard of no
sketohing.' /s/ M. Churchill, Brigadier General."

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
-8
The following quotation is from a letter from the Acting
Wernor-General of the Philippine Islands, Charles E. Yeater, to
the Seeretary of War, under date of June 5, 1919, regarding a visit
he had made to Malampaya Sound.
"Among other things, he (Captain Wallace) said that about
three weeks before our arrival a small Japanese ship dropped
anohor in the Sound and staid about a week. There were several
Japanese oivilians on board, very well dressed, and they were
very nioe to all the natives who wished to go on the vessel, and
entertained them with lantern slides and phonograph musio. On
his return from one of his lumber camps, the Captain found them
there and asked what they were interested in in that part of the
Island. They replied that they had heard that there was gold
there and they were investigating the report. He asked if they
had found any, and they said they thought they had found traces
on the west side of the inner harbor. The Captain also told us
that they went all around the Sound and took numerous photo-
graphs."
It is considered that such visits by Japanese vessels,
taking of soundings in known harbors, eto., is directly contrary
to our interests and should not be allowed.
It is suggested that a report on this question be received
from the Governor of the Philippine Islands, and that if con-
ditions warrant, suggestions be made to the Japanese Government
to discontine such visits in the future.
Very truly yours,
Acting Seoretary of the Navy.
The Honorable
The Seoretary of State.

HONOLULU, HAWAII
May 18, 1939.
The Commissioner of Customs,
Treasury Department,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
There is enclosed herewith for the information of the
Bureau, a copy of areport dated May 9, 1939, submitted to
this office, covering the activities around the Japanese
Naval Tanker HINS ERI O, which arrived at this port on May 3,
1939.
Under date of April 8, 1939, Stephen B. Gibbons, As-
sistant Secretary of the Treasury, wrote this offioe author-
izing the extension of the "usual customs courtesies and
facilities in connection with the arrival of the Japanese Trans-
port Erimo at your port from April 30 to May 3, for the purpose
of replenishing its supply of water and food".
The information given in the att ched copy of the Inspeotor's
report has been gathered primarily at the request of the Naval
Intelligence Service at this port and was obtained from the vessel
and persons named therein, who visited the vessel during its stay.
All information was seoured by observatin or was given willingly
and at no time did the Inspector gathering the information attempt
to secure such information, except through courteous means.
It is believed that your office will find the information
intere:ting as the report is very comprehensive and thorough
and does load to some question as to whether suoh extended ac-
tivity described therein should be permitted in view of the
wording of the Assistant Secretary's letter.
For example, on the second page of the report under the
heading - "Local Japanese Community", there is listed many arti-
cles that were given to the vessel by people of Japanese ancestry
residing in Honolulu among which is listed 8 cases of sorap tin,
7 cases of tinfoil, 1 ton of scrap iron and 25 bags of cotton rags.
The practice at this port relating to these vessels is to
place a guard at the foot of the gangway only for the purpose of
preventing the landing of any dutiable merohandise without proper
entry. Otherwise no attempt has been made to interfere with any
arrangements between the vessel and persons of the local Japanese
community.
Your comment on the practice at this port in this matter and
any instructions would be appreciated by this office.
Respeotfully,
(Signed) Warde C. Hiberly,
Aoting Collector of Customs.

894.3311/556
This Department rs tn roceipt of a communication from the Secretary
of the Treasury, wherewith were transmitted copies of correspondence
received by him from the Collector of Customs at Honolulu, T. H.,
dealing with the arrival and departure of the Japanese Naval Tanker
HIS MRDMO, at that port in May of this year.
The Department is tranamitting herewith copies of this correspondence,
from which it will be observed thet the ERIMO took on board screp
iron and other cargo, ostensibly for discharge in Japan.
Under the provisions of Section 4197 R. S., as amended, the master
of any vessel departing for a foreign port is required to file a
manifest of all cargo on board his craft, and to obtain a clearance
from the collector of customs at the port of departure. If the
master fails to file a menifest and obtein a clearence, he becames
personally l1able to a penalty of not less than $500 nor more
than $1,000.
In the instant case, the vessel being a public vessel of Japan,
the Department 1s of opinion that the circumstances of the case
should be brought to your attention for such disposition as you may
determine.
This is the second occasion within the last year in which public
vessels of Japan have taken on cargo in Hawaii without complying
with the above noted statute. The SHINTOkU MARU on a training
cruise, on or about August 24, 1938, was laden at Hilo, T. H.
with scrap iron and general merchandise under the same circumstances
as the Naval Tanker HI.S ERIMO.
This Department will be pleased to be informed of your decision when
the same has been reached.
Cordighly yours
J. M. Johnsn,
Englosures
Assistantypecretary of Commerce.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
All entertainment of the personnel while
ashore was sponsored by the Japanese Chamber of Commerce
and all programs ashore conducted and directed by Mr. Oi
and Mr. Hamamoto.
The United States Marines that tied up the vessel
when it arrived were invited to attend the "At Rome" program.
Those that attended were: Privates Pesely, Garrison, Plummer,
Speight, Mauzey and Tucker from the Marine Detachment, Old
Naval Station, Ala Maona, Honolulu, T. D.
INCIDENTS OF NOTE
No soundinss were taken when the vessel was arriving
or departing.
Outside of Government Agencies only mombers of the
Japanese race were freely admitted aboard the vessel.
With the exception of two members all of the ship's
personnel have had experience under action in China. This
information was given by the Captain to visitors aboard the
vessel.
As far as could be ascertained all provisions and
supplies taken aboard the vessel in this port was contributed
by the local Japanese.
The "mustang" officers (ones not graduates of the
Japanese Naval Acadomy) seldom returned salutes from sailors
below the grade of petty officer.
The sailors were armed with German Mauser rifles,
.3ll-inch calibre.

COMMUNIC/TIONS
Divi AND LEOORDS

FA EASTERN A
ADVISER
OCT 311939
The Secretary of State.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Sir:
Further reference is made to your letter of August 9, 1939 (Le
894.3311/556), enclosing a copy of a communication dated July 24,
1939, from the Department of Commerce concerning certain cargo laden
on the Japanese Naval Tanker HIMS ERIMO during its call at the port
of Honolulu from May 3 to May 6, 1939.
In the communication of the Department of Commerce referred to
it is stated that under the provisions of section 4197 of Rev. Stat.
of 1878, as amended (U.S.C., Sup. IV, title 46, sec. 91), the master
006.6232
of any vessel departing for a foroign port is required to file a
manifest of all cargo on board his craft and to obtain a clearance
from the collector of customs at the port of departure. If the
master fails to file a manifest and obtain a clearance, he becomes
611.94232
personally liable to a penalty of not less than $500 nor more than
$1,O00. It is further stated that this is the second occasion within
the last year in wnich a public vessel of Japan has taken on a cargo
without complying with the above noted statute. You request the
opinion of this Department on the question raised by the Department
of Commerce.
The statute referred to is one administered by the Department
of Commerce and customs officers act only as agents for that De-
partment in requiring manifests and shippers' export declarations
to cover cargoes laden on outward bound vessels. It is therefore
the opinion of the Treasury Department that the question as to
whether a manifest and shipper's export declaration should be re-
quired for cargo laden on a public vessel of a foreign government
to which customs courtesies and facilities have been extended is
one which must necessarily be decided by the Department of Commerce
and this Department does not in the circumstances feel varranted
in expressing an opinion on the question. Further, the Treasury
Department extends customs courtesies and facilities to such vessels
FIFG
only at the request of the Department of State. The Department

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
515.31
- 2 -
feels therefore that the question as to whether the lading of cargo
on a foreign government vessel to which customs courtesies and
facilities have been extended is an abuse of the privileges ex-
tended is one wich should be decided by the Department of State.
Thore is enclosed for your information a copy of this Depart-
ment's communication of February 28, 1939, to tne Honorable J. M.
Johnson, Assistant Secretary of Commeroe, giving tne Treasury De-
partment's interpretation of the term "usual customs courtesies
and facilities".
As requested in the last paragraph of your letter the collector
of customs at Honolulu is being directed to report as to whether
the scrap tin laden on the HIMS ERINO was tin-plate scrap within
the meaning of paragraph 1 of the reguiations promulgated December
7, 1936, by the Department of State pursuant to the Executive Order
of February 16, 1936, issued by the President under the authority
tested in him by the Act of February 15, 1936, 49 Stat. 1140
(U.S.C., Sup. IV, title 50, secs. 86 to 88, inclusive). When the
collector's reply is receivod, you will be advised as to whether
the scrap tin laden on the HIMS ERI'O was tin-plate scrap within
the meaning of the regulations referred to.
A copy of this communication is being forvarded to the
Secretary of Commerce for nis information and any action deemed

Very truly yours,

Sir:
In compliance with the request contained in your com-
munication of September 23, 1939 (PR 894.3311/560). the
collector of customs at Honolulu, T. H., is being author-
ized in a communioation of even date as to the changes in
dates of the visits to Honolulu and Hilo of the Japanese
894.3311/574
coast defense ships YAKUMO and IWATE of the Japanese Train-
ing Squadron and the transport SHIRITOKO.
The Department's authorizations to the collectors of
customs at San Francisco, California, and Los Angeles,
California, that the usual customs courtesies and facilities
be extended on the arrival of these ships at the ports of
San Pedro and San Francisco have been revoked.
By direction of the Secretary:
Very truly yours,

A mumber of compatriots and myself as peaceful citizens of
the United States, wish to protest against the placing of armed
sentries of a foreign nation on United States soil.
894.3311
This was done here in Hilo recently during the visit of the
Japanese training ships, Iwate and Yakumo, and since armed sentries
were never placed during previous visits by Japanese or ships of other
nations it is believed that the Japanese are trying to force subserv-
ience upon the residents here similarly as they did in China and which
methods were recently publicay deplored by Ambassador J. Grew.
The fact that thousands of local residents, former immigrants
and their offspring to the second and third generation, and also city
894.3311/585
and sounty officials visiting the ships, submitted to an ordeal of dust-
ing their shoes and brushing them with a disinfectant and washing their
hands ir disinfectant and bowing low to an armed sentry in a sentry-box
on United States soil, clearly establishes the failure of the local
commurity in instilling American ideals irto a great mumber of its res-
idents.
A newspaper clipping which refers to an ircident involving
an official of the United States is submitted.
Respectfully yours,
Anmo Hecethaln

The Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
In re Le 894.3311/566
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The Department is in receipt of your letter of November 1, 1959,
whereim you refer to its letter of July 24, 1939, in connection
with the action of two public vessels of Japan in taking on, at
894.3311/586
ports of the United States, scrap iron and other cargo, ostensibly
for discharge in Japan.
It is noted that you are of the opinion that the customs courtesies
extended to foreign public vessels clearly do not warrant such
vessels lading miscellaneous cargo without compliance with the
provisions of Section 4197, R.S., as amended, and that when such
vessels take aboard miscellaneous cargo destined for a fereign
port, without complying with the provisions of Section 4l97, R.S.,
as mended, such action constitutes a clear abuse of the customs
courtesies extended to such vessels, and should not be permitted.
It is further noted that, in view of the time which has elapsed
since the visit of the two Japanese vessels under reference, you
deem it inadvisable at this time to bring the matter of their
violation of American laws formally to the attention of the
Japanese government.
This Department is in full accord with the views expressed in
the penultimate paragraph of your letter, and will issue appro-
priate instructions to its field offices immediately, to the
effect that if any foreign public vessel attempts to take on
cargo for discharge at a foreign port, without complying with
the provisions of Section 4197, R. S., as amended, or any other.
applicable law or regulation, the commander of such vessel shall
be informed that full compliance with such requirements is
mandatory.
The Department's advices also will contain additional instructions
to the offect that if the commander of the foreign public vessel
F/FG
593
607

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.95)
Pago 2
The Secretary of State
November 15, 1959
disregards such notification, the incident should be reported
to this Department immediately, in order that it, in turn,
can tranmit the information to you, for appropriate
representations to the foreign government concerned.
Cordially yours,
Mohuin
JM. Johpon
Assistany Secretary of Commerce

diplomatic missions are required to obtain
the permission of this Government for visits
of public vessels of their respeotive
countries to visit even open ports of this
country. PR may wish to draft an

of the Department in cases of this kind.

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
December 12, 1939
 TiYokohama's despatoh 574, November 4,
1939, entitled "Calls of Japanese Public
Vessels at United States Ports and the
Question of Reciprocity Regarding Prior
Authorization for such Visits".
The Consul at Yokohama herein submits
a tabulation of the voyages of Japanese
publio vessels from Yokohama or Yokosuka to
Àmerioan ports, compiled from his records of
the granting of bills of health. During 1938
and the first 10 months of 1939, a period of
22 months altogether, he granted bills of
health for sixteen such voyages.
"Most of the vessels are either so-called
training ships or naval tankers and are
operated by the Navy or by the Department
of Eduoation in a majority of instances.
Certain naval tankers are used to maintain
an oil supply service for the Japanese Navy.
"Purposes of these trips are seldom an-
nounoed and the only statement which 1s
made is that the vessels are 'training ships'
or 'special service ships (naval tankers)'."
"An 1nteresting sidelight is the fact
that seven calls were made at Hilo. This is
of course not warranted by its importance
as a port. An attempt has been made to dis-
cover the exact reasons for this but the
most that can be adduced is that owing to
the

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2
the fairly large Japanese population there,
the men are feted and like the reception
which they receive at Hilo."
The particular fact to which the Consul
direots attention 1s that he does not generally
require authorization from the United States
Government before 1ssuing bills of health for
these voyages, and he points out that the
Japanese Government 1n all cases requires the
obtaining of special authorization from the
Japanese Government before American public ves-
sels are allowed to visit any Japanese port.
The Consul points out that it is only when the
Japanese public vessel expects to visit an
American closed port (e.g. 
Pribilof Islands) that the Consulate requires
previous authorization from the Department of
State before authenticating or issuing the
bills of health.
894.3311/589

Purpose of trips. It is seldom that any official
pronouncement is made regarding the purpose of such
visits. According to the procedure generally followed,
a routine notification is sent to the Consulate from
the

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the Yokosuka Naval Station saying that an officer will
call at a certain time (usually two days after writing)
for authentication of signature on a bill of health
from Yokosuka. A medical officer brings in the certificate
and an interpreter attempts to get information for our
records since these men almost invariably do not speak
English.
We may surmise that the naval tankers are bringing
oil for the Japanese Navy in most instances. One of
the few cases in which really definite statements re-
garding the purpose of the voyage were submitted was
that of the Fisheries Institute ship HAkUYO MARU which
cruised in Alaskan waters earlier this year. The matter
was the subject of correspondence between this office
and the Consulate General and it was terminated only
after a telegraphic instruction arrived from the Depart-
ment stating that permission for this cruise had been
granted. In the correspondence the Japanese Government
declared that the vessel was visiting the Pribilof Is-
lands to study fur sealing and Dutch Harbor for a visit.
On all other occasions the vessels have been described
merely as "training ships" or "special service ships
(naval tankers)".
Authorization. The question of autiorization from
the United States Government is an open one. The only
time this Consulate requires previous authorization
from the Department of State before authenticating or
issuing

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
issuing bills of health to Japanese government vessels
is when such vessels wish to touch at closed ports,
such as Dutch Harbor. When the ships are clearing for
open ports no advance authorization has been required.
This seems to be at variance with the Japanese
requirement that advance permission be given by them
before American government vessels, such as the supply
ship U.S.S. GOLD STAR and other United States naval
vessels, can touch at any open Japanese port.
Distribution of calls. It is noteworthy that cer-
tain ports are favored by these government ships. Hawaii
is a destination frequently chosen. Honolulu leads
all other ports, ten calls having been made there between
January 1, 1938 - October 31, 1939. This is understandable
since even tiose ships bound for the west coast of the
United States would normally stop there.
An interesting sidelight is the fact that seven
calls were made at Hilo. This is of course not warranted
by its importance as a port. An attempt has been made
to discover the exact reasons for this but the most that
can be adduced is that owing to the fairly large Japanese
population there, the men are feted and like the recep-
tion which they receive at Hilo.
Attention may be called to the fact that out of a
total of thirty calls made by Japanese public vessels
during the period under review, twenty-three were at
ports where concentration of Japanese population is
greatest

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
greatest: Honolulu, ten calls; Hilo, seven calls; Los
Angeles, six calls.
Summary. Evidence of advance authorization from
1
the United States Government for cruises by Japanese
public vessels to American ports has not been required
at the Consulate except when such ships wish to visit
closed ports. The Japanese Government, on the other
hand, requires authorization in each instance for calls
by naval vessels of the United States at open ports in
Japan.
Sixteen voyages have been made by Japanese government
ships during the past twenty-two months. Most or the
vessels are either so-called training ships or naval
tankers and are operated by the Navy or by the Department
of Education in a majority of instances. Certain naval
tankers are used to maintain an oil supply service for
the Japanese Navy.
Purposes of these trips are seldom announced and
the only statement which is made is that the vessels
are "training ships" or "special service ships (naval
tankers)".
In the course of the sixteen voyages, thirty calls
have been made at American ports: Hawaii, seventeen;
United States, west coast, eleven; Alaska, two. Hawaii
is a favored destination (Honolulu, ten calls; Hilo,
seven calls). It may also be noted that twenty-three
out of the total of thirty calls were at ports where
concentration of Japanese population is greatest:
Honolulu,

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.96)
-7 -
Honolulu, ten; Hilo, seven; Los Angeles, six.
Respectfully yours,
ianardao
Richard F. Boyce,
American Consul.

JLG/gh
